\lambda_{1, 2} = \frac{T \pm \sqrt{T^2 - 4D}}{2}
$$
We analyze two mutually exclusive cases:
1. **Case 1: Complex conjugate eigenvalues ($T^2 - 4D < 0$):**
   The discriminant is negative, so the roots are complex conjugates:
   $$
   \lambda_{1, 2} = \frac{T}{2} \pm i \frac{\sqrt{4D - T^2}}{2}
   $$
   The real part of both eigenvalues is:
   $$
   \operatorname{Re}(\lambda) = \frac{T}{2}
   $$
   For asymptotic stability, we require $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda) < 0$, which is equivalent to $T < 0$. Furthermore, because $4D > T^2 \ge 0$, we automatically have $D > 0$.
2. **Case 2: Real eigenvalues ($T^2 - 4D \ge 0$):**
   Both roots $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$ are real.
   - If both roots are strictly negative ($\lambda_1 < 0, \lambda_2 < 0$), their sum is negative and their product is positive:
     $$
     T = \lambda_1 + \lambda_2 < 0
     $$
     $$
     D = \lambda_1 \lambda_2 > 0
     $$
   - Conversely, assume $T < 0$ and $D > 0$. Since $D = \lambda_1 \lambda_2 > 0$, both eigenvalues have the same sign. Since their sum $T = \lambda_1 + \lambda_2 < 0$, that shared sign must be strictly negative. Thus $\lambda_1 < 0$ and $\lambda_2 < 0$.

Combining both cases, $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_1) < 0$ and $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_2) < 0$ if and only if $\operatorname{tr}(A) < 0$ and $\det(A) > 0$.

## Second-Order Differential Equations and Companion Matrices

### Reduction of Order

In mechanical and electrical systems, physical laws (such as Newton's second law $F = m a$ or Kirchhoff's laws) naturally generate second-order differential equations:
$$
m \frac{d^2 y}{dt^2} + b \frac{dy}{dt} + k y = 0
$$
where $m > 0$ represents mass (or inductance $L$), $b \ge 0$ represents damping (or resistance $R$), and $k > 0$ represents spring stiffness (or reciprocal capacitance $1/C$).

Dividing by the mass $m$ (or setting $m = 1$ without loss of generality):
$$
y'' + b y' + k y = 0 \iff y'' = -k y - b y'
$$
To apply linear algebra, convert this single second-order scalar equation into a system of two first-order equations by introducing the velocity state variable:
$$
u(t) = \begin{bmatrix} y(t) \\ y'(t) \end{bmatrix}
$$
The first derivative of the state vector is:
$$
\frac{du}{dt} = \begin{bmatrix} y' \\ y'' \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} y' \\ -k y - b y' \end{bmatrix}
$$
In matrix form:
$$
\frac{d}{dt} \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -k & -b \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix}
$$
The matrix $A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -k & -b \end{bmatrix}$ is the **companion matrix** of the second-order equation.

### Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors of the Companion Matrix

Compute the characteristic polynomial of $A$:
$$
\det(A - \lambda I) = \det \begin{bmatrix} -\lambda & 1 \\ -k & -b - \lambda \end{bmatrix} = (-\lambda)(-b - \lambda) - (1)(-k) = \lambda^2 + b \lambda + k = 0
$$
This reproduces the classical characteristic equation obtained by substituting $y(t) = e^{\lambda t}$ into $y'' + b y' + k y = 0$:
$$
(\lambda^2 + b \lambda + k) e^{\lambda t} = 0 \implies \lambda^2 + b \lambda + k = 0
$$
To find the eigenvectors $x = \begin{bmatrix} x_1 & x_2 \end{bmatrix}^T$, solve $(A - \lambda I) x = 0$:
$$
\begin{bmatrix} -\lambda & 1 \\ -k & -b - \lambda \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} \implies -\lambda x_1 + x_2 = 0 \implies x_2 = \lambda x_1
$$
Choosing $x_1 = 1$, the eigenvector corresponding to $\lambda$ is:
$$
x = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \lambda \end{bmatrix}
$$
When the roots $\lambda_1 \ne \lambda_2$ are distinct, the state vector solution is:
$$
u(t) = \begin{bmatrix} y(t) \\ y'(t) \end{bmatrix} = c_1 e^{\lambda_1 t} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \lambda_1 \end{bmatrix} + c_2 e^{\lambda_2 t} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ \lambda_2 \end{bmatrix}
$$
The top component gives the position $y(t) = c_1 e^{\lambda_1 t} + c_2 e^{\lambda_2 t}$, while the bottom component automatically gives the velocity $y'(t) = c_1 \lambda_1 e^{\lambda_1 t} + c_2 \lambda_2 e^{\lambda_2 t}$.

### Physical Regimes of Second-Order Systems

The discriminant $\Delta = b^2 - 4mk$ governs the nature of the roots $\lambda_{1, 2} = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4mk}}{2m}$:

| Regime | Condition | Eigenvalues $\lambda_{1, 2}$ | Dynamics |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Undamped** | $b = 0$ | Pure imaginary: $\pm i \sqrt{k/m}$ | Pure sinusoidal oscillation at natural frequency $\omega_n = \sqrt{k/m}$; closed circular/elliptical orbits in phase space. |
| **Underdamped** | $b^2 < 4mk$ | Complex conjugate: $-\frac{b}{2m} \pm i \omega_d$ | Damped oscillations decaying like $e^{-\frac{b}{2m}t}$; spirals inward toward the origin in phase space. |
| **Critically Damped** | $b^2 = 4mk$ | Repeated real root: $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = -\frac{b}{2m}$ | Fastest non-oscillatory return to equilibrium; companion matrix is defective. |
| **Overdamped** | $b^2 > 4mk$ | Distinct real roots: $\lambda_2 < \lambda_1 < 0$ | Monotonic, non-oscillatory decay dominated asymptotically by the slower mode $e^{\lambda_1 t}$. |

### Undamped Motion and Phase Plane Geometry

For an undamped harmonic oscillator ($m = 1, b = 0, k = 1$), the equation is $y'' + y = 0$.
The companion system is:
$$
\frac{d}{dt} \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix}
$$
The system matrix $A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ is skew-symmetric ($A^T = -A$).
Its eigenvalues are $\lambda = \pm i$, with eigenvectors $x_1 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ i \end{bmatrix}$ and $x_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -i \end{bmatrix}$.

Starting from initial conditions $y(0) = 1, y'(0) = 0$, the exact solution is:
$$
u(t) = \begin{bmatrix} y(t) \\ y'(t) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \cos t \\ -\sin t \end{bmatrix}
$$
In the $(y, y')$ phase plane, the trajectory satisfies:
$$
\|u(t)\|^2 = y(t)^2 + (y'(t))^2 = \cos^2 t + \sin^2 t = 1
$$
The state vector traces a circle of radius 1 with period $2\pi$, representing continuous conservation of total mechanical energy:
$$
E = \frac{1}{2} k y^2 + \frac{1}{2} m (y')^2 = \text{constant}
$$

## Defective Matrices and Repeated Eigenvalues

When an $n \times n$ matrix $A$ has repeated eigenvalues whose geometric multiplicity is strictly less than their algebraic multiplicity, $A$ lacks a full basis of $n$ independent eigenvectors. In this scenario, $A$ cannot be diagonalized ($A \ne X \Lambda X^{-1}$), and the pure modal expansion $\sum c_i e^{\lambda_i t} x_i$ is incomplete.

### Matrix Exponential via Nilpotent Decomposition

Consider a matrix $A$ with a repeated eigenvalue $\lambda$ of algebraic multiplicity 2 and geometric multiplicity 1. We decompose $A$ into its scalar multiple of the identity and a traceless nilpotent operator $N$:
$$
A = \lambda I + (A - \lambda I) = \lambda I + N
$$
where $N = A - \lambda I$.

By the Cayley-Hamilton theorem, because $\det(A - \mu I) = (\mu - \lambda)^2$, the matrix $N$ satisfies:
$$
N^2 = (A - \lambda I)^2 = 0
$$
Because the scalar identity matrix $\lambda I$ commutes with every matrix, $[\lambda I, N] = 0$. Therefore, the exponential splits into a product:
$$
e^{At} = e^{(\lambda I + N)t} = e^{\lambda t I} e^{Nt}
$$
The scalar matrix exponential is:
$$
e^{\lambda t I} = e^{\lambda t} I
$$
Because $N^2 = 0$, the infinite power series for $e^{Nt}$ terminates identically after the linear term:
$$
e^{Nt} = I + Nt + \frac{1}{2!} N^2 t^2 + \dots = I + Nt = I + (A - \lambda I)t
$$
Therefore, the exact closed-form matrix exponential is:
$$
e^{At} = e^{\lambda t} \big( I + (A - \lambda I)t \big)
$$

> [!theorem] Solution for Defective $2 \times 2$ Systems
> For any $2 \times 2$ matrix $A$ with repeated eigenvalue $\lambda$ satisfying $(A - \lambda I)^2 = 0$, the solution to $\frac{du}{dt} = Au$ is:
> $$
> u(t) = e^{At} u(0) = e^{\lambda t} \big( I + (A - \lambda I)t \big) u(0) = e^{\lambda t} u(0) + t e^{\lambda t} (A - \lambda I)u(0)
> $$

This provides a direct, rigorous linear algebraic derivation of why the secular factor $t e^{\lambda t}$ arises in differential equations with repeated roots: it is the linear truncation of the nilpotent power series for $e^{Nt}$.

## Numerical Discretization and Difference Equations

When differential equations cannot be solved analytically, or when integrating on a computer, continuous equations $\frac{du}{dt} = Au$ are discretized into difference equations $U_{n+1} = M U_n$. The choice of discretization controls the stability and geometric fidelity of the numerical solution.

### Discretization of the Harmonic Oscillator

Consider the oscillator $y'' = -y$, or $\frac{du}{dt} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} u$ where $u = \begin{bmatrix} Y \\ Z \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix}$. Let $\Delta t$ denote the discrete time step, with $Y_n \approx y(n \Delta t)$ and $Z_n \approx y'(n \Delta t)$.

The second derivative can be approximated by finite differences:
$$
\frac{Y_{n+1} - 2Y_n + Y_{n-1}}{(\Delta t)^2} = -Y_*
$$
The choice of time index $*$ on the right side produces three classic numerical schemes:
1. **Forward (Explicit) Euler ($* = n-1$):**
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   Y_{n+1} &= Y_n + \Delta t \, Z_n \\
   Z_{n+1} &= Z_n - \Delta t \, Y_n
   \end{aligned}
   \implies U_{n+1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ -\Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_n
   $$
2. **Backward (Implicit) Euler ($* = n+1$):**
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   Y_{n+1} - \Delta t \, Z_{n+1} &= Y_n \\
   Z_{n+1} + \Delta t \, Y_{n+1} &= Z_n
   \end{aligned}
   \implies \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\Delta t \\ \Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_{n+1} = U_n
   $$
3. **Centered / Leapfrog Scheme ($* = n$):**
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   Y_{n+1} &= Y_n + \Delta t \, Z_n \\
   Z_{n+1} &= Z_n - \Delta t \, Y_{n+1}
   \end{aligned}
   \implies \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ \Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_{n+1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_n
   $$
4. **Trapezoidal (Crank-Nicolson) Method:**
   Averaging forward and backward differences:
   $$
   \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\frac{\Delta t}{2} \\ \frac{\Delta t}{2} & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_{n+1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \frac{\Delta t}{2} \\ -\frac{\Delta t}{2} & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_n
   $$

### Stability and Eigenvalue Analysis of Numerical Operators

After $n$ steps, the discrete state is $U_n = M^n U_0$, where $M$ is the amplification matrix. Stability requires that the eigenvalues $\lambda$ of $M$ satisfy $|\lambda| \le 1$:

1. **Forward Euler Instability:**
   For $M = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ -\Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix}$, the eigenvalues are $\lambda = 1 \pm i \Delta t$.
   Their magnitude is:
   $$
   |\lambda| = \sqrt{1^2 + (\Delta t)^2} > 1 \quad \text{for any } \Delta t > 0
   $$
   Because $|\lambda| > 1$, powers $M^n$ grow without bound: numerical trajectories spiral outwards, blowing up exponentially.
2. **Backward Euler Dissipation:**
   For $M = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\Delta t \\ \Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix}^{-1}$, the eigenvalues are $\lambda = \frac{1}{1 \pm i \Delta t}$.
   Their magnitude is:
   $$
   |\lambda| = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + (\Delta t)^2}} < 1 \quad \text{for any } \Delta t > 0
   $$
   The scheme introduces artificial numerical damping: trajectories spiral inwards toward the origin.
3. **Leapfrog / Symplectic Stability:**
   Inverting the left matrix for the leapfrog scheme gives:
   $$
   U_{n+1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ -\Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} U_n = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ -\Delta t & 1 - (\Delta t)^2 \end{bmatrix} U_n
   $$
   The determinant is $\det(M) = 1(1 - (\Delta t)^2) - (\Delta t)(-\Delta t) = 1$.
   The trace is $\operatorname{tr}(M) = 2 - (\Delta t)^2$.
   The eigenvalues satisfy $\lambda^2 - (2 - (\Delta t)^2)\lambda + 1 = 0$.
   For $|\operatorname{tr}(M)| \le 2$, which requires:
   $$
   -2 \le 2 - (\Delta t)^2 \le 2 \iff 0 \le \Delta t \le 2
   $$
   the eigenvalues form complex-conjugate pairs on the unit circle: $|\lambda_1| = |\lambda_2| = 1$. The method is neutrally stable and preserves area in phase space. If $\Delta t > 2$, $|\lambda| > 1$, and the scheme explodes.
4. **Trapezoidal Orthogonality (Cayley Transform):**
   The amplification matrix is:
   $$
   M = \left( I - \frac{\Delta t}{2} B \right)^{-1} \left( I + \frac{\Delta t}{2} B \right)
   $$
   where $B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ is skew-symmetric ($B^T = -B$).
   Because $B^T = -B$, $M$ is an exact orthogonal matrix ($M^T M = I$). Therefore:
   $$
   \|U_{n+1}\|^2 = U_{n+1}^T U_{n+1} = U_n^T M^T M U_n = U_n^T U_n = \|U_n\|^2
   $$
   The points $U_n$ remain exactly on the circle for all time steps $\Delta t$, exhibiting unconditional stability and exact energy preservation.

## Nonhomogeneous Systems and Particular Solutions

### The Forced Differential Equation

When an external driving force $f(t)$ acts on the system, the equation becomes nonhomogeneous:
$$
\frac{du}{dt} = Au + f(t), \quad u(0) = u_0
$$
By linearity, the complete solution is the sum of the homogeneous (nullspace) solution $u_n(t)$ and a particular solution $u_p(t)$:
$$
u(t) = u_n(t) + u_p(t)
$$
where $\frac{d u_n}{dt} = A u_n$ with $u_n(0) = u_0$, and $\frac{d u_p}{dt} = A u_p + f(t)$ with $u_p(0) = 0$.

### Derivation via Integrating Factor (Variation of Parameters)

> [!theorem] Variation of Parameters Formula
> The general solution to the forced linear system $\frac{du}{dt} = Au + f(t)$ is:
> $$
> u(t) = e^{At} u(0) + \int_0^t e^{A(t-s)} f(s) \, ds
> $$

**Proof:**
Multiply both sides of $\frac{du}{dt} - Au = f(t)$ on the left by the integrating factor $e^{-At}$:
$$
e^{-At} \frac{du}{dt} - e^{-At} A u = e^{-At} f(t)
$$
Using the product rule and the identity $\frac{d}{dt}\left( e^{-At} \right) = -A e^{-At} = -e^{-At} A$:
$$
\frac{d}{dt}\left( e^{-At} u(t) \right) = e^{-At} \frac{du}{dt} - e^{-At} A u(t)
$$
Therefore:
$$
\frac{d}{dt}\left( e^{-At} u(t) \right) = e^{-At} f(t)
$$
Integrate both sides with respect to dummy variable $s$ from $s = 0$ to $s = t$:
$$
\int_0^t \frac{d}{ds}\left( e^{-As} u(s) \right) ds = \int_0^t e^{-As} f(s) \, ds
$$
Applying the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus:
$$
e^{-At} u(t) - e^0 u(0) = \int_0^t e^{-As} f(s) \, ds
$$
Since $e^0 = I$, add $u(0)$ to both sides:
$$
e^{-At} u(t) = u(0) + \int_0^t e^{-As} f(s) \, ds
$$
Multiply on the left by $(e^{-At})^{-1} = e^{At}$:
$$
u(t) = e^{At} u(0) + e^{At} \int_0^t e^{-As} f(s) \, ds
$$
Because the matrix $e^{At}$ is constant with respect to the integration variable $s$, it moves inside the integral:
$$
e^{At} e^{-As} = e^{A(t-s)}
$$
Thus:
$$
u(t) = e^{At} u(0) + \int_0^t e^{A(t-s)} f(s) \, ds
$$

> [!note] Physical Interpretation of Duhamel's Integral
> The particular solution $u_p(t) = \int_0^t e^{A(t-s)} f(s) \, ds$ represents a continuous superposition of responses to infinitesimal impulses:
> 1. An impulse of force $f(s) \, ds$ is applied at time $s$.
> 2. This input propagates forward over the remaining elapsed time interval $(t - s)$ governed by the system propagator $e^{A(t-s)}$.
> 3. Integrating over $s \in [0, t]$ accumulates the contributions of all past inputs up to the present time $t$.

### Constant Forcing Term

If the external input is constant, $f(t) = b$, and $A$ is invertible ($\lambda_i \ne 0$), the steady-state particular solution is found by setting $\frac{du_p}{dt} = 0$:
$$
0 = A u_p + b \implies u_p = -A^{-1} b
$$
Evaluating the integral directly confirms this:
$$
u_p(t) = \int_0^t e^{A(t-s)} b \, ds = \left[ -A^{-1} e^{A(t-s)} b \right]_{s=0}^{s=t} = (-A^{-1} I b) - (-A^{-1} e^{At} b) = (e^{At} - I) A^{-1} b
$$

## Transition to Partial Differential Equations: Heat and Wave Equations

When a spatial continuum is discretized at $n$ discrete grid points, linear partial differential equations (PDEs) reduce directly to systems of ordinary differential equations $\frac{du}{dt} = Au$ or $\frac{d^2 u}{dt^2} = Au$.

### The Heat Equation (Diffusion)

The one-dimensional heat equation is:
$$
\frac{\partial u}{\partial t} = \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}
$$
Discretizing the spatial domain with spacing $\Delta x$ using the second centered difference:
$$
\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}\Big|_{x_j} \approx \frac{u_{j+1} - 2u_j + u_{j-1}}{(\Delta x)^2}
$$
Under Dirichlet boundary conditions ($u_0 = u_{n+1} = 0$), this generates the semi-discrete linear system:
$$
\frac{du}{dt} = Au
$$
where $A$ is the symmetric, negative definite tridiagonal second-difference matrix:
$$
A = \frac{1}{(\Delta x)^2} \begin{bmatrix} -2 & 1 & 0 & \dots & 0 \\ 1 & -2 & 1 & \dots & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & -2 & \dots & 0 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \dots & 1 & -2 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Because $A$ is symmetric and negative definite:
1. All eigenvalues of $A$ are strictly negative real numbers: $\lambda_j < 0$.
2. All modes decay exponentially: $e^{\lambda_j t} \to 0$ as $t \to \infty$.
3. Higher-frequency modes (eigenvectors with rapid sign changes) correspond to large negative eigenvalues and decay fastest, smoothing out spatial spikes over time.

### The Wave Equation (Oscillation)

The one-dimensional wave equation is:
$$
\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial t^2} = \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}
$$
Spatial discretization produces a second-order vector differential equation:
$$
\frac{d^2 u}{dt^2} = Au
$$
with the same second-difference matrix $A$.

Substitute a modal trial solution $u(t) = e^{i \omega t} x$:
$$
\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\left( e^{i \omega t} x \right) = (i \omega)^2 e^{i \omega t} x = -\omega^2 e^{i \omega t} x
$$
Substitute into the equation:
$$
-\omega^2 e^{i \omega t} x = e^{i \omega t} Ax \implies Ax = -\omega^2 x
$$
Thus, $x$ must be an eigenvector of $A$ with eigenvalue:
$$
\lambda = -\omega^2
$$
Because $A$ is negative definite, all eigenvalues $\lambda$ are negative real numbers ($\lambda < 0$). Therefore:
$$
\omega = \sqrt{-\lambda} > 0
$$
is purely real. The solutions are unattenuated standing oscillations:
$$
u(t) = (c_1 \cos \omega t + c_2 \sin \omega t) x
$$
Energy is strictly conserved, and the system oscillates indefinitely without dissipation.

## Numerical Computation of Eigenvalues: The $QR$ Algorithm

Computing eigenvalues by constructing the characteristic polynomial $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ and finding its roots is numerically unstable and highly ill-conditioned for matrices of order $n \ge 4$ (due to Wilkinson's phenomenon). Practical numerical linear algebra relies on iterative similarity transformations.

### Hessenberg Reduction

A full matrix $A$ is first reduced to **upper Hessenberg form** $H$ (where all entries below the first subdiagonal are zero: $h_{ij} = 0$ for $i > j + 1$) via a sequence of orthogonal Householder reflections or Givens rotations:
$$
A_0 = Q_0^T A Q_0 = H
$$
If $A$ is symmetric, $H$ is both symmetric and Hessenberg, which makes $H$ tridiagonal.

### The Iterative $QR$ Step

Given the Hessenberg matrix $A_k$, each iteration performs a $QR$ factorization and reverses the matrix factors:
1. **Factor:** Compute the $QR$ decomposition of $A_k$:
   $$
   A_k = Q_k R_k
   $$
   where $Q_k$ is orthogonal ($Q_k^T Q_k = I$) and $R_k$ is upper triangular.
2. **Reverse:** Form the next iterate by reversing the product:
   $$
   A_{k+1} = R_k Q_k
   $$

> [!theorem] Similarity of $QR$ Iterates
> Every iterate $A_{k+1}$ is orthogonally similar to $A_k$:
> $$
> A_{k+1} = Q_k^{-1} A_k Q_k = Q_k^T A_k Q_k
> $$
> Consequently, all iterates $A_k$ share identical eigenvalues.

**Proof:**
From the factorization $A_k = Q_k R_k$, multiply on the left by $Q_k^{-1}$:
$$
R_k = Q_k^{-1} A_k
$$
Substitute this expression for $R_k$ into the reversed product:
$$
A_{k+1} = R_k Q_k = (Q_k^{-1} A_k) Q_k = Q_k^{-1} A_k Q_k = Q_k^T A_k Q_k
$$
Since $A_{k+1}$ is related to $A_k$ by an orthogonal similarity transformation, their characteristic polynomials, determinants, traces, and eigenvalues are identical.

### Convergence and Shifts

Under the $QR$ iteration, the subdiagonal elements of $A_k$ converge rapidly to zero:
$$
A_k \longrightarrow T \quad \text{as } k \to \infty
$$
where $T$ is an upper triangular matrix (or block upper triangular in the presence of complex conjugate eigenvalues). The eigenvalues of $A$ appear directly on the main diagonal of $T$.

To accelerate convergence from linear to cubic, a spectral shift $s_k$ is subtracted before factoring:
$$
\begin{aligned}
A_k - s_k I &= Q_k R_k \\
A_{k+1} &= R_k Q_k + s_k I
\end{aligned}
$$
Choosing $s_k$ close to an eigenvalue (such as the Rayleigh quotient or the Wilkinson shift from the trailing $2 \times 2$ block) yields cubic convergence for symmetric matrices.

## Problem Set 6.5 Solutions

### Exercise 3: Continuous Markov Systems and Zero Eigenvalues

**Problem:**
(a) If every column of $A$ adds to zero, why is $\lambda = 0$ an eigenvalue?
(b) With negative diagonal and positive off-diagonal entries adding to zero in each column, $u' = Au$ is a continuous Markov equation. Find the eigenvalues, eigenvectors, and the steady state as $t \to \infty$ for:
$$
\frac{du}{dt} = \begin{bmatrix} -2 & 3 \\ 2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} u \quad \text{with } u(0) = \begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$
What is $u(\infty)$, and what conservation law does the system satisfy?

*Solution:*
**(a)** Let $\mathbf{1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & \dots & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ be the row vector of all ones. The $j$-th entry of the row vector $\mathbf{1} A$ is the sum of the entries in the $j$-th column of $A$:
$$
(\mathbf{1} A)_j = \sum_{i=1}^n a_{ij}
$$
If every column of $A$ adds to zero, then:
$$
\mathbf{1} A = \mathbf{0} = 0 \cdot \mathbf{1}
$$
Transposing both sides yields:
$$
A^T \mathbf{1}^T = 0 \cdot \mathbf{1}^T
$$
This demonstrates that $\mathbf{1}^T$ is an eigenvector of $A^T$ corresponding to the eigenvalue $\lambda = 0$. Because a square matrix and its transpose share identical characteristic polynomials ($\det(A - \lambda I) = \det(A^T - \lambda I)$), they possess identical eigenvalues. Therefore, $\lambda = 0$ must also be an eigenvalue of $A$.

**(b)** For the matrix $A = \begin{bmatrix} -2 & 3 \\ 2 & -3 \end{bmatrix}$:
1. **Eigenvalues:**
   The trace is $\operatorname{tr}(A) = -2 + (-3) = -5$.
   The determinant is $\det(A) = (-2)(-3) - (3)(2) = 6 - 6 = 0$.
   The characteristic equation is:
   $$
   \lambda^2 - \operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda + \det(A) = \lambda^2 + 5\lambda = \lambda(\lambda + 5) = 0
   $$
   The eigenvalues are $\lambda_1 = 0$ and $\lambda_2 = -5$.

2. **Eigenvectors:**
   - For $\lambda_1 = 0$:
     $$
     (A - 0I)x_1 = \begin{bmatrix} -2 & 3 \\ 2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_{11} \\ x_{21} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} \implies -2x_{11} + 3x_{21} = 0 \implies x_1 = \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix}
     $$
   - For $\lambda_2 = -5$:
     $$
     (A + 5I)x_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 3 & 3 \\ 2 & 2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_{12} \\ x_{22} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} \implies x_{12} + x_{22} = 0 \implies x_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{bmatrix}
     $$

3. **General Solution and Initial Conditions:**
   The general solution is:
   $$
   u(t) = c_1 e^{0 \cdot t} \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix} + c_2 e^{-5t} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{bmatrix} = c_1 \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix} + c_2 e^{-5t} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Evaluating at $t = 0$:
   $$
   c_1 \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix} + c_2 \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Adding the two rows: $5c_1 = 5 \implies c_1 = 1$.
   Substituting $c_1 = 1$ into the first row: $3(1) + c_2 = 4 \implies c_2 = 1$.
   The exact trajectory is:
   $$
   u(t) = \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix} + e^{-5t} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$

4. **Steady State and Invariance:**
   As $t \to \infty$, $e^{-5t} \to 0$. The steady-state equilibrium is:
   $$
   u(\infty) = \lim_{t \to \infty} u(t) = \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Furthermore, summing the components shows that the total quantity is strictly invariant:
   $$
   u_1(t) + u_2(t) = (3 + e^{-5t}) + (2 - e^{-5t}) = 5 = u_1(0) + u_2(0) \quad \text{for all } t \ge 0
   $$

### Exercise 12: Defective Systems and Emergence of Secular Modes

**Problem:**
Substitute $y = e^{\lambda t}$ into the second-order differential equation $y'' = 6y' - 9y$ to show that $\lambda = 3$ is a repeated root. Show that the companion matrix for $u = \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $\lambda = 3, 3$ with only a one-dimensional eigenspace. Verify that $y(t) = t e^{3t}$ is a second linearly independent solution, and explain how the secular term $t e^{\lambda t}$ emerges directly from the matrix exponential of a defective matrix.

*Solution:*
1. **Characteristic Roots:**
   Substituting $y = e^{\lambda t}$ into $y'' - 6y' + 9y = 0$:
   $$
   (\lambda^2 - 6\lambda + 9)e^{\lambda t} = 0 \implies (\lambda - 3)^2 = 0 \implies \lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = 3
   $$
   The algebraic multiplicity of $\lambda = 3$ is $2$.

2. **Companion System and Defectiveness:**
   Defining state vector $u = \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix}$, the first-order system is:
   $$
   \frac{du}{dt} = \begin{bmatrix} y' \\ y'' \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -9 & 6 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} y \\ y' \end{bmatrix} = Au
   $$
   The characteristic polynomial of the companion matrix $A$ is:
   $$
   \det(A - \lambda I) = \det \begin{bmatrix} -\lambda & 1 \\ -9 & 6 - \lambda \end{bmatrix} = \lambda^2 - 6\lambda + 9 = (\lambda - 3)^2 = 0
   $$
   To determine the eigenspace, solve $(A - 3I)x = 0$:
   $$
   \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 1 \\ -9 & 3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} \implies -3x_1 + x_2 = 0 \implies x_2 = 3x_1
   $$
   Every eigenvector is a scalar multiple of $x = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{bmatrix}$. The geometric multiplicity is $1 < 2$, so $A$ lacks a complete basis of eigenvectors and is defective.

3. **Verification of the Secular Mode:**
   Let $y_2(t) = t e^{3t}$. Differentiating via the product rule:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   y_2'(t) &= (1 + 3t)e^{3t} \\
   y_2''(t) &= 3e^{3t} + 3(1 + 3t)e^{3t} = (6 + 9t)e^{3t}
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Substituting into the differential operator:
   $$
   y_2'' - 6y_2' + 9y_2 = (6 + 9t)e^{3t} - 6(1 + 3t)e^{3t} + 9(te^{3t}) = (6 - 6 + (9 - 18 + 9)t)e^{3t} = 0
   $$
   Since the ratio $y_2(t) / y_1(t) = (t e^{3t}) / e^{3t} = t$ is non-constant, $y_1(t) = e^{3t}$ and $y_2(t) = t e^{3t}$ are linearly independent, giving the general solution $y(t) = c_1 e^{3t} + c_2 t e^{3t}$.

4. **Matrix Exponential Origin of Secular Growth:**
   Decompose $A$ into its scalar eigenvalue part and nilpotent generator: $A = 3I + N$, where:
   $$
   N = A - 3I = \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 1 \\ -9 & 3 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Computing $N^2$:
   $$
   N^2 = \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 1 \\ -9 & 3 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} -3 & 1 \\ -9 & 3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 9 - 9 & -3 + 3 \\ 27 - 27 & -9 + 9 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Because $3I$ and $N$ commute ($(3I)N = N(3I) = 3N$), the matrix exponential factors exactly:
   $$
   e^{At} = e^{(3I + N)t} = e^{3It} e^{Nt} = (e^{3t} I)(I + Nt + \mathbf{0}) = e^{3t} \begin{bmatrix} 1 - 3t & t \\ -9t & 1 + 3t \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   The linear polynomial factor $t$ multiplying $e^{3t}$ emerges fundamentally from the truncation of the nilpotent series $e^{Nt} = I + Nt$.

### Exercise 14: Skew-Symmetric Systems, Norm Preservation, and Orthogonal Exponentials

**Problem:**
The system matrix $A$ is skew-symmetric ($A^T = -A$):
$$
\frac{du}{dt} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & c & -b \\ -c & 0 & a \\ b & -a & 0 \end{bmatrix} u \iff \begin{aligned} u_1' &= c u_2 - b u_3 \\ u_2' &= a u_3 - c u_1 \\ u_3' &= b u_1 - a u_2 \end{aligned}
$$
(a) Prove that the Euclidean norm is preserved for all time, $\|u(t)\|^2 = \|u(0)\|^2$, both by direct component substitution and via the matrix product $\frac{d}{dt}(u^T u)$.
(b) Prove from the power series that $Q(t) = e^{At}$ is an orthogonal matrix ($Q^T = Q^{-1}$) for all $t \in \mathbb{R}$.

*Solution:*
**(a) Energy and Norm Conservation:**
- **Componentwise Verification:**
  Differentiating $\|u(t)\|^2 = u_1^2 + u_2^2 + u_3^2$ with respect to time:
  $$
  \frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|^2 = 2u_1 u_1' + 2u_2 u_2' + 2u_3 u_3'
  $$
  Substituting the differential equations:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  \frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|^2 &= 2u_1(c u_2 - b u_3) + 2u_2(a u_3 - c u_1) + 2u_3(b u_1 - a u_2) \\
  &= 2(c u_1 u_2 - b u_1 u_3 + a u_2 u_3 - c u_1 u_2 + b u_1 u_3 - a u_2 u_3) \\
  &= 0
  \end{aligned}
  $$
- **Matrix Derivation:**
  Using the product rule on the inner product $u^T u$:
  $$
  \frac{d}{dt}(u^T u) = (u')^T u + u^T u' = (Au)^T u + u^T (Au) = u^T A^T u + u^T A u = u^T (A^T + A) u
  $$
  Since $A$ is skew-symmetric, $A^T = -A$, which implies $A^T + A = 0$. Therefore:
  $$
  \frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|^2 = u^T \mathbf{0} u = 0 \implies \|u(t)\|^2 = \|u(0)\|^2 \quad \text{for all } t \ge 0
  $$

**(b) Orthogonality of the Matrix Exponential:**
Taking the transpose of the infinite power series definition of $Q = e^{At}$:
$$
Q^T = \left( \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{(At)^k}{k!} \right)^T = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{(A^T t)^k}{k!} = e^{A^T t}
$$
Substituting $A^T = -A$:
$$
Q^T = e^{-At}
$$
Because the matrices $At$ and $-At$ commute ($At(-At) = (-At)At = -A^2 t^2$), their exponentials combine by the additive exponent rule:
$$
Q^T Q = e^{-At} e^{At} = e^{-At + At} = e^{\mathbf{0}} = I
$$
Similarly, $Q Q^T = e^{At} e^{-At} = I$.
Thus, $Q^T = Q^{-1}$, proving that $Q(t) = e^{At}$ is an orthogonal matrix for all $t$. The flow generates a continuous rotation (isometry) in $\mathbb{R}^n$, keeping the state on the sphere $\|u(t)\| = \|u(0)\|$.

### Exercise 20: Non-Commutativity of Matrix Exponentials

**Problem:**
In general, $e^A e^B \ne e^B e^A$, and neither equals $e^{A+B}$ unless $A$ and $B$ commute ($AB = BA$).
Verify this non-commutativity explicitly for the matrices:
$$
A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad A + B = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Show that $AB \ne BA$ and compute $e^A$, $e^B$, $e^{A+B}$, $e^A e^B$, and $e^B e^A$.

*Solution:*
1. **Commutator $[A, B]$:**
   Multiply $A$ and $B$:
   $$
   AB = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   $$
   BA = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
   $$
   Since $AB \ne BA$, the matrices do not commute ($[A, B] \ne 0$).

2. **Compute $e^A$:**
   Notice that $A^2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = A$.
   Because $A$ is idempotent ($A^k = A$ for all $k \ge 1$), the series sums directly:
   $$
   e^A = I + \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{A^k}{k!} = I + A \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{1}{k!} = I + (e - 1)A
   $$
   Evaluating explicitly:
   $$
   e^A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} + (e - 1) \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} e & 4(e - 1) \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$

3. **Compute $e^B$:**
   Notice that $B^2 = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -4 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \mathbf{0}$.
   Because $B$ is nilpotent ($B^k = \mathbf{0}$ for $k \ge 2$), the series truncates:
   $$
   e^B = I + B = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$

4. **Compute $e^{A+B}$:**
   The sum is diagonal:
   $$
   A + B = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \implies e^{A+B} = \begin{bmatrix} e^1 & 0 \\ 0 & e^0 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} e & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
   $$

5. **Compare the Products:**
   - Multiply $e^A e^B$:
     $$
     e^A e^B = \begin{bmatrix} e & 4(e - 1) \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} e & -4e + 4(e - 1) \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} e & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
     $$
   - Multiply $e^B e^A$:
     $$
     e^B e^A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} e & 4(e - 1) \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} e & 4(e - 1) - 4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} e & 4e - 8 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
     $$

Comparing the three resulting matrices:
$$
e^{A+B} = \begin{bmatrix} e & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad e^A e^B = \begin{bmatrix} e & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad e^B e^A = \begin{bmatrix} e & 4e - 8 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Because $-4 \ne 4e - 8 \ne 0$, all three matrices are distinct:
$$
e^A e^B \ne e^B e^A \ne e^{A+B}
$$
This demonstrates that the identity $e^{A+B} = e^A e^B$ fails whenever matrices do not commute.

### Exercise 22: Non-Singularity and Invertibility of the Matrix Exponential

**Problem:**
Give two distinct mathematical proofs that the matrix exponential $e^{At}$ is strictly non-singular for every square matrix $A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ and all $t \in \mathbb{R}$:
(a) By explicitly constructing its two-sided matrix inverse using the power series.
(b) By applying the Spectral Mapping Theorem and Jacobi's formula for the determinant.

*Solution:*
**(a) Algebraic Inverse Construction:**
For any matrix $A$ and scalar $t$, define the matrix $M = e^{-At}$ via the convergent series:
$$
M = e^{-At} = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{(-At)^k}{k!}
$$
Because the matrices $At$ and $-At$ commute ($At(-At) = -A^2 t^2 = (-At)At$), their exponentials multiply according to the additive exponent law:
$$
e^{At} M = e^{At} e^{-At} = e^{At + (-At)} = e^{\mathbf{0}} = I
$$
Similarly, $M e^{At} = e^{-At} e^{At} = I$.
Because there exists an explicit two-sided inverse $(e^{At})^{-1} = e^{-At}$, the matrix $e^{At}$ is invertible, which proves it is non-singular ($\operatorname{rank}(e^{At}) = n$).

**(b) Spectral Mapping and Jacobi's Formula:**
Let $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_n$ denote the eigenvalues of $A$ (counted with algebraic multiplicity).
By the Spectral Mapping Theorem, if $\lambda_i$ is an eigenvalue of $A$ with eigenvector $x_i$, then:
$$
e^{At} x_i = \left( \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{t^k A^k}{k!} \right) x_i = \left( \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{t^k \lambda_i^k}{k!} \right) x_i = e^{\lambda_i t} x_i
$$
Thus, the eigenvalues of $e^{At}$ are precisely $e^{\lambda_1 t}, e^{\lambda_2 t}, \dots, e^{\lambda_n t}$.
The determinant of a matrix equals the product of its eigenvalues:
$$
\det(e^{At}) = \prod_{i=1}^n e^{\lambda_i t} = \exp\left( \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i t \right) = \exp\big( \operatorname{tr}(A)t \big)
$$
Because the scalar exponential function satisfies $e^z \ne 0$ for all finite complex numbers $z \in \mathbb{C}$:
$$
\det(e^{At}) = e^{\operatorname{tr}(A)t} \ne 0 \quad \text{for all } A \text{ and all } t \in \mathbb{R}
$$
A matrix with a non-zero determinant is non-singular.

### Exercise 23 & 24: Leapfrog Discretization, Symplectic Area Preservation, and Critical Stability Threshold

**Problem:**
The centered second-difference equation $Y_{n+1} - 2Y_n + Y_{n-1} = -(\Delta t)^2 Y_n$ for the simple harmonic oscillator $y'' = -y$ can be written as the first-order Leapfrog system:
$$
\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ \Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} Y_{n+1} \\ Z_{n+1} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} Y_n \\ Z_n \end{bmatrix}
$$
(a) Invert the left-hand matrix to find the one-step transition matrix $A$ in $U_{n+1} = A U_n$. Prove that $\det(A) = 1$ for all $\Delta t$, confirming that Leapfrog is an area-preserving (symplectic) map.
(b) For $\Delta t = 1$, compute the eigenvalues of $A$, verify that $|\lambda_1| = |\lambda_2| = 1$, and prove that $A^6 = I$, meaning the discrete orbit has exact period $6$.
(c) Derive the characteristic equation for general $\Delta t$. Evaluate the eigenvalues for $\Delta t = \sqrt{2}$ and the critical threshold case $\Delta t = 2$.
(d) Prove that for any time step $\Delta t > 2$, the eigenvalues become real with $|\lambda| > 1$, causing exponential instability ($U_n \to \infty$). Conclude that $\Delta t \le 2$ is the exact stability threshold.

*Solution:*
**(a) Transition Matrix and Symplecticity:**
The left matrix is $L = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ \Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ with $\det(L) = 1$, and its inverse is:
$$
L^{-1} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ -\Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Multiplying by the right-hand matrix $R = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ gives the transition matrix $A$:
$$
A = L^{-1} R = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ -\Delta t & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ -\Delta t & 1 - (\Delta t)^2 \end{bmatrix}
$$
The determinant of $A$ is:
$$
\det(A) = \det(L^{-1}) \det(R) = \left( \frac{1}{1} \right)(1) = 1
$$
Because $\det(A) = 1$ identically for all $\Delta t$, the map preserves phase-space area, satisfying the geometric property of symplectic integrators.

**(b) Periodic Behavior at $\Delta t = 1$:**
For $\Delta t = 1$:
$$
A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ -1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
$$
The trace is $\operatorname{tr}(A) = 1$ and $\det(A) = 1$. The characteristic equation is:
$$
\lambda^2 - \lambda + 1 = 0 \implies \lambda_{1, 2} = \frac{1 \pm i\sqrt{3}}{2} = e^{\pm i\pi / 3}
$$
Both eigenvalues lie on the unit circle: $|\lambda_1| = |\lambda_2| = 1$.
Because $\lambda^6 = (e^{\pm i\pi / 3})^6 = e^{\pm 2\pi i} = 1$, diagonalizable powers satisfy:
$$
A^6 = X \Lambda^6 X^{-1} = X I X^{-1} = I
$$
Thus, $U_6 = A^6 U_0 = U_0$, and the discrete trajectory forms an exact closed orbit of period $6$.

**(c) General Spectral Analysis:**
For arbitrary step size $\Delta t$, the trace of $A$ is $\operatorname{tr}(A) = 2 - (\Delta t)^2$ and $\det(A) = 1$.
The characteristic equation is:
$$
\lambda^2 - \big( 2 - (\Delta t)^2 \big)\lambda + 1 = 0
$$
- For $\Delta t = \sqrt{2}$: $\operatorname{tr}(A) = 2 - 2 = 0 \implies \lambda^2 + 1 = 0 \implies \lambda_{1, 2} = \pm i$. The orbit is neutrally stable with period $4$ ($A^4 = I$).
- For $\Delta t = 2$: $\operatorname{tr}(A) = 2 - 4 = -2 \implies \lambda^2 + 2\lambda + 1 = (\lambda + 1)^2 = 0 \implies \lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = -1$.
  Here $A + I = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 2 \\ -2 & -2 \end{bmatrix} \ne \mathbf{0}$, so $A$ is defective and powers grow linearly like $n(-1)^n$.

**(d) Instability Threshold for $\Delta t > 2$:**
For $\Delta t > 2$, let $\gamma = (\Delta t)^2 > 4$. The trace is $T = 2 - \gamma < -2$.
The discriminant of the quadratic equation is:
$$
\Delta_{\text{disc}} = T^2 - 4 = (2 - \gamma)^2 - 4 = \gamma^2 - 4\gamma = \gamma(\gamma - 4) > 0
$$
Because the discriminant is strictly positive, the eigenvalues are purely real and distinct:
$$
\lambda_{1, 2} = \frac{T \pm \sqrt{T^2 - 4}}{2}
$$
Since $\lambda_1 \lambda_2 = \det(A) = 1$, the roots are mutual reciprocals: $\lambda_2 = 1 / \lambda_1$.
Since $T < -2$, both roots are negative, and for the smaller root:
$$
\lambda_2 = \frac{T - \sqrt{T^2 - 4}}{2} < \frac{-2 - 0}{2} = -1 \implies |\lambda_2| > 1
$$
Because $|\lambda_2| > 1$, the power $A^n$ contains an exponentially growing term $|\lambda_2|^n \to \infty$.
Therefore, the Leapfrog integrator is stable if and only if:
$$
\Delta t \le 2
$$
Step sizes exceeding $\Delta t = 2$ lead to catastrophic exponential divergence.

### Exercise 25: The Cayley Transform and Unconditional Stability of Trapezoidal Integration

**Problem:**
The implicit trapezoidal (Crank-Nicolson) scheme for $y'' = -y$ is:
$$
\begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\Delta t / 2 \\ \Delta t / 2 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} Y_{n+1} \\ Z_{n+1} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t / 2 \\ -\Delta t / 2 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} Y_n \\ Z_n \end{bmatrix}
$$
(a) Invert the left matrix to express $U_{n+1} = A U_n$. Show that $A$ is an orthogonal matrix ($A^T A = I$) for every step size $\Delta t > 0$, proving that the numerical state $U_n$ remains exactly on the circle $\|U_n\| = \|U_0\|$.
(b) Prove the general Cayley Transform theorem: if $B$ is any skew-symmetric matrix ($B^T = -B$), then $A = (I - B)^{-1} (I + B)$ is unconditionally an orthogonal matrix.

*Solution:*
**(a) Orthogonality and Energy Preservation:**
Let $\delta = \frac{\Delta t}{2}$ and $B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$. The system is $(I - \delta B) U_{n+1} = (I + \delta B) U_n$.
The left matrix is:
$$
I - \delta B = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -\delta \\ \delta & 1 \end{bmatrix} \implies (I - \delta B)^{-1} = \frac{1}{1 + \delta^2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \delta \\ -\delta & 1 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Multiplying by $I + \delta B = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \delta \\ -\delta & 1 \end{bmatrix}$:
$$
A = \frac{1}{1 + \delta^2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \delta \\ -\delta & 1 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \delta \\ -\delta & 1 \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{1 + \delta^2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 - \delta^2 & 2\delta \\ -2\delta & 1 - \delta^2 \end{bmatrix}
$$
Computing $A^T A$:
$$
A^T A = \frac{1}{(1 + \delta^2)^2} \begin{bmatrix} 1 - \delta^2 & -2\delta \\ 2\delta & 1 - \delta^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 - \delta^2 & 2\delta \\ -2\delta & 1 - \delta^2 \end{bmatrix}
$$
The diagonal entries are:
$$
(1 - \delta^2)^2 + (-2\delta)^2 = 1 - 2\delta^2 + \delta^4 + 4\delta^2 = (1 + \delta^2)^2
$$
The off-diagonal entries are:
$$
(1 - \delta^2)(2\delta) + (-2\delta)(1 - \delta^2) = 0
$$
Therefore:
$$
A^T A = \frac{1}{(1 + \delta^2)^2} \begin{bmatrix} (1 + \delta^2)^2 & 0 \\ 0 & (1 + \delta^2)^2 \end{bmatrix} = I
$$
Because $A$ is an orthogonal matrix, multiplication by $A$ preserves the vector norm:
$$
\|U_{n+1}\|^2 = (A U_n)^T (A U_n) = U_n^T (A^T A) U_n = U_n^T I U_n = \|U_n\|^2
$$
The discrete solution remains exactly on the circle $\|U_n\| = \|U_0\|$ for all $n \ge 0$, regardless of the time step $\Delta t$.

**(b) General Proof via the Cayley Transform:**
Let $B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be any skew-symmetric matrix ($B^T = -B$).
The Cayley transform of $B$ is defined by:
$$
A = (I - B)^{-1} (I + B)
$$
*(Note: Because the eigenvalues of real skew-symmetric matrices are purely imaginary, $\det(I - B) \ne 0$, guaranteeing $I - B$ is always invertible.)*

Observe that factors $(I - B)$ and $(I + B)$ commute:
$$
(I - B)(I + B) = I + B - B - B^2 = I - B^2 = (I + B)(I - B)
$$
Multiplying by $(I - B)^{-1}$ on both left and right yields:
$$
(I + B)(I - B)^{-1} = (I - B)^{-1}(I + B)
$$
Now compute the transpose of $A$:
$$
A^T = \big( (I - B)^{-1} (I + B) \big)^T = (I + B)^T \big( (I - B)^{-1} \big)^T = (I + B^T)(I - B^T)^{-1}
$$
Substituting $B^T = -B$:
$$
A^T = (I - B)(I + B)^{-1}
$$
Now evaluate the matrix product $A^T A$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
A^T A &= \big( (I - B)(I + B)^{-1} \big) \big( (I - B)^{-1}(I + B) \big) \\
&= (I - B) \big( (I + B)^{-1}(I - B)^{-1} \big) (I + B) \\
&= (I - B) \big( (I - B)(I + B) \big)^{-1} (I + B) \\
&= (I - B) (I - B^2)^{-1} (I + B)
\end{aligned}
$$
Because $(I - B)(I + B) = I - B^2$, we have $(I - B) = (I - B^2)(I + B)^{-1}$. Substituting:
$$
A^T A = (I - B^2)(I + B)^{-1} (I - B^2)^{-1} (I + B) = I
$$
Thus $A^T A = I$, proving that the Cayley transform maps every skew-symmetric matrix to an orthogonal matrix unconditionally.

### Exercise 26: Three Proofs of the Exponent Identity $(e^A)^2 = e^{2A}$

**Problem:**
Provide three distinct mathematical proofs that $(e^A)^2 = e^{2A}$ for any square matrix $A$:
1. Via the dynamical flow (semigroup property) of $\frac{du}{dt} = Au$.
2. Via squaring the infinite power series using the Cauchy product and the binomial theorem.
3. Via spectral decomposition and diagonalization $A = X \Lambda X^{-1}$.

*Solution:*
**Proof 1 (Dynamical System Flow):**
Consider the autonomous system $\frac{du}{dt} = Au$ with initial state $u(0) = u_0$.
- Integrating from $t = 0$ to $t = 1$, the fundamental solution operator yields:
  $$
  u(1) = e^A u(0)
  $$
- By time-invariance, evolving from $t = 1$ to $t = 2$ over an elapsed duration of $1$ unit applies the propagator $e^A$ to the state $u(1)$:
  $$
  u(2) = e^A u(1) = e^A \big( e^A u(0) \big) = (e^A)^2 u(0)
  $$
- Alternatively, integrating directly from $t = 0$ to $t = 2$ in a single stage yields:
  $$
  u(2) = e^{2A} u(0)
  $$
Because $(e^A)^2 u_0 = e^{2A} u_0$ holds for every initial vector $u_0 \in \mathbb{C}^n$, the operators must be identical:
$$
(e^A)^2 = e^{2A}
$$

**Proof 2 (Series Expansion and Cauchy Product):**
Expand $e^A$ using its infinite power series:
$$
e^A = \sum_{j=0}^\infty \frac{A^j}{j!}
$$
Forming the square $(e^A)^2$ via the Cauchy product of absolutely convergent series:
$$
(e^A)^2 = \left( \sum_{j=0}^\infty \frac{A^j}{j!} \right) \left( \sum_{m=0}^\infty \frac{A^m}{m!} \right) = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \left( \sum_{j=0}^k \frac{A^j}{j!} \frac{A^{k-j}}{(k-j)!} \right)
$$
Because powers of the same matrix commute ($A^j A^{k-j} = A^k$), we apply the binomial coefficient identity:
$$
\sum_{j=0}^k \frac{A^j}{j!} \frac{A^{k-j}}{(k-j)!} = \frac{1}{k!} \sum_{j=0}^k \frac{k!}{j!(k-j)!} A^k = \frac{1}{k!} \left( \sum_{j=0}^k \binom{k}{j} \right) A^k = \frac{(1 + 1)^k}{k!} A^k = \frac{2^k A^k}{k!} = \frac{(2A)^k}{k!}
$$
Summing over all $k \ge 0$:
$$
(e^A)^2 = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{(2A)^k}{k!} = e^{2A}
$$

**Proof 3 (Spectral Decomposition via Diagonalization):**
Assume first that $A$ is diagonalizable: $A = X \Lambda X^{-1}$, where $\Lambda = \operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_n)$.
Then $e^A = X e^\Lambda X^{-1}$. Squaring $e^A$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(e^A)^2 &= (X e^\Lambda X^{-1})(X e^\Lambda X^{-1}) \\
&= X e^\Lambda (X^{-1} X) e^\Lambda X^{-1} \\
&= X (e^\Lambda)^2 X^{-1}
\end{aligned}
$$
Because $e^\Lambda$ is diagonal, its square is obtained by squaring each scalar diagonal entry:
$$
(e^\Lambda)^2 = \operatorname{diag}(e^{\lambda_1}, \dots, e^{\lambda_n})^2 = \operatorname{diag}\big( (e^{\lambda_1})^2, \dots, (e^{\lambda_n})^2 \big) = \operatorname{diag}(e^{2\lambda_1}, \dots, e^{2\lambda_n}) = e^{2\Lambda}
$$
Substituting back:
$$
(e^A)^2 = X e^{2\Lambda} X^{-1} = e^{X (2\Lambda) X^{-1}} = e^{2A}
$$
*(Note: Because diagonalizable matrices are dense in $\mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ and the mapping $A \mapsto (e^A)^2 - e^{2A}$ is continuous, the identity $(e^A)^2 = e^{2A}$ holds for all square matrices, including defective matrices.)*
